# HairCS: Reconstructing Strand-Based Hair from Hair Cards

ZIXUAN LU<sup>†</sup>, University of Utah, United States of America TONGTONG WANG, LIGHTSPEED, Australia YUEFAN SHEN, LIGHTSPEED, China ZHONGTIAN ZHENG, LIGHTSPEED, China CHENFANFU JIANG, UCLA, United States of America YIN YANG, University of Utah, United States of America KUI WU, LIGHTSPEED, United States of America

![](images/0dff0cb083a426b12d727c598ba15ec85482c6979211d803bb59069800f10bef.jpg)  
Fig. 1. We present a pipeline that automatically converts a hair-card model into a high-quality strand-based hairstyle. Ourfive-step pipeline begins with guide extraction and constructs a wrapper geometry that captures the wisp’s volumetric occupancy. The resulting strands are compatible with grooming operators and ready for strand-based simulation and rendering in industrial game engines. From left to right: hair cards rendered with their textures, the bare card geometry, our output strand-based model, and our result enriched with the curly operator.

Durin<sub>g</sub> renderin<sub>g</sub>, these reduced re<sub>p</sub>resentati<sub>ons are</sub> t<sub>yp</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y expan</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>ures or</sub> <sub>proce</sub>d<sub>ura</sub>l f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> A<sub>mong</sub> th<sub>em,</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r</sub> <sub>car</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>w</sub>id<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>use</sub>d in the <sub>g</sub>amin<sub>g</sub> industr<sub>y</sub> due to their sim<sub>p</sub>licit<sub>y</sub> and eficienc<sub>y</sub> [Jian<sub>g</sub> 2016]. We refer readers to the course b<sub>y</sub> Bertails et al. [2008] for a com<sub>p</sub>rehensive overview of hair renderin<sub>g</sub> and simulation. More <sub>recen</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>ore</sub>d <sub>neura</sub>l <sub>represen-</sub> tations for reconstructin<sub>g</sub> and re<sub>p</sub>resentin<sub>g</sub> com<sub>p</sub>lex hair <sub>g</sub>eometr<sub>y</sub> [Luo et al. 2024; Wan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2023; Zhen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2025a].

Hair Modeling. Hair models are traditionally created by artists usin<sub>g</sub> tools such as Ma<sub>y</sub>a XGen<sub>,</sub> but manual authorin<sub>g</sub> remains laborintensive. To sim<sub>p</sub>lif<sub>y</sub> this <sub>p</sub>rocess, Yuksel et al. [2009] introduced hair mesh, a volumetric mesh representation that provides highl<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>e</sub>diti<sub>ng con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>genera</sub>t<sub>es s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d<sub>s proce</sub>d<sub>ura</sub>ll<sub>y w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> mesh. Wu et al. [2024a] <sub>p</sub>resented a <sub>g</sub>eometric method for modelin<sub>g</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> <sub>co</sub>il<sub>e</sub>d h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>es</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub>f<sub>ros.</sub> M<sub>ore</sub> <sub>recen</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> H<sub>uang</sub> <sub>e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> [2025] <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osed a real-time framework that d<sub>y</sub>namicall<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s hair i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> thi<sub>c</sub>k<sub>, camera-</sub>f<sub>ac</sub>i<sub>ng car</sub>d<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or ren</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> T<sub>o re</sub>d<sub>uce</sub> th<sub>e e</sub>f<sub>or</sub>t re<sub>q</sub>uired to author hair cards, Zhen<sub>g</sub> et al. [2025b] and Tojo et al. [2025] introduced automatic <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>elines that convert strand-based h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o car</sub>d<sub>s us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e ren</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> Ch<sub>ang e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> [2025] <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osed an inverse hair <sub>g</sub>roomin<sub>g p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline that transforms 3D <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o proce</sub>d<sub>ura</sub>l h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r grooms</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a sma</sub>ll <sub>se</sub>t of <sub>g</sub>uide strands and hair <sub>g</sub>roomin<sub>g</sub> o<sub>p</sub>erators.

Hair Generation. Sketch-based interfaces provide intuitive control f<sub>or</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>e</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>users</sub> t<sub>o</sub> d<sub>raw</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub> <sub>screen</sub> s<sub>p</sub>ace [Fu et al. 2007; Shen et al. 2020]. For automatic hair <sub>g</sub>eneration, <sub>many</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>recons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d<sub>-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>geome</sub>t<sub>ry</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> i<sub>mages.</sub> Earlier a<sub>pp</sub>roaches relied on heuristics [Hu et al. 2017; Jakob et al. 2009; Kong and Nakajima 1998; Paris et al. 2008; Sun et al. 2021] or lar<sub>g</sub>e 3D hairst<sub>y</sub>le databases [Chai et al. 2016; Hu et al. 2015; Lian<sub>g</sub> et al. 2018]. More recent learnin<sub>g</sub>-based methods have im<sub>p</sub>roved the fidelit<sub>y</sub> and robustness of reconstruction from sin<sub>g</sub>le-view [Wu <sub>e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2022<sub>;</sub> Zh<sub>an an</sub>d Zh<sub>en</sub> 2019<sub>;</sub> Zh<sub>en e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2023<sub>;</sub> Zh<sub>ou e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2018] and multi-view in<sub>p</sub>uts [Kuan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2022; Rosu et al. 2022; Skl<sub>yarova e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2023<sub>;</sub> T<sub>a</sub>ki<sub>mo</sub>t<sub>o e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2024<sub>;</sub> W<sub>u e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>.</sub> 2024b<sub>;</sub> Z<sub>a</sub>kh<sub>arov</sub> et al. 2024; Zhou et al. 2024]. Be<sub>y</sub>ond reconstruction, hair s<sub>y</sub>nthesis methods <sub>p</sub>erform hairst<sub>y</sub>le transfer usin<sub>g</sub> feature ma<sub>p</sub>s [Wan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2009] and, more recentl<sub>y</sub>, learned features [Chen et al. 2024; He et al. 2025a; Skl<sub>y</sub>arova et al. 2024; Zhou et al. 2023]. However, these l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s o</sub>ft<sub>en re</sub>l<sub>y on s</sub>l<sub>ow op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on proce</sub>d<sub>ures,</sub> or their out<sub>p</sub>ut <sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> is limited b<sub>y</sub> inade<sub>q</sub>uate re<sub>p</sub>resentations<sub>,</sub> such <sub>as</sub> l<sub>ow-reso</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on or</sub> d<sub>ens</sub>it<sub>y gr</sub>id<sub>s, an</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e res</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d hairst<sub>y</sub>le diversit<sub>y</sub> and <sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> of the trainin<sub>g</sub> data.

[2015] introduced USC-HairSalon, the fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>pu</sub>bli<sub>c</sub>l<sub>y access</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> 3D h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>e</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub>b<sub>ase</sub> f<sub>or</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a-</sub>d<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ven</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>li<sub>ng researc</sub>h<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t h<sub>as suppor</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>li<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>genera</sub>ti<sub>on researc</sub>h f<sub>or more</sub> th<sub>an a</sub> d<sub>eca</sub>d<sub>e an</sub>d h<sub>as ena</sub>bl<sub>e</sub>d <sub>nu-</sub> merous follow-u<sub>p</sub> studies [He et al. 2025a; Rosu et al. 2025; Wu et al. 2024b; Zhou et al. 2023]. Our Pipeline. Fig. 2 illustrates our overall pipeline. We begin by extracting guide hairs (guides) from the input hair cards (Sec. 4.1), and then bind their roots to uniforml<sub>y</sub> sam<sub>p</sub>led scal<sub>p p</sub>ositions (Sec. 4.2). ![](images/d9d07f238d5a6ef3998fab91cfba97151f801fe6e3f730300b280e18ecb82623.jpg)  
Fig. 2. Our pipeline. Given textured triangle strips as hair cards, (a) textured and (b) pure geometry, we extract each card’s flow orientation, root side, and centerline, then bind the resulting guides to root candidates on the scalp. (c) shows the binding relaxation, with bound segments highlighted in red. The set ofunbound roots is then sparsified, and a group ofextra guides is added to cover the entire scalp. The root-connected guides are then smoothed to form the final guide set (d). We construct a wrapper and partition it via an expansion algorithm (e), then synthesize our base hair within the wrapper (f). The resulting base hair can be edited into a variety ofstyles, such as curly hair, via the groom modifier (g).

![](images/3d449fb7f1dcc873a1878bc06feb5f420160ac434fa587e16b23fb95f8603b4a.jpg)  
Fig. 3. Card orientation analysis. The high-pass filtered grayscale texture of each card is passed through the orientation analysis, which yields the root side ( ), the tip side ( ), evenly spaced root samples ( ), and the flow direction ( → ). The oriented 2D information is then lifted into the 3D shape.

![](images/4512dc57e70a0506358b536638878b0d3ab43088653941d91944cf96bbfbc31a.jpg)  
Fig. 5. Hair parting. Given the input hair cards and their extracted centerlines (a), each centerline is bound to the scalp and inherits an orientation vector projected onto the surface (b). We then construct a Voronoi tessellation on the scalp seeded by the bound roots, and identify candidate partition edges as those whose two adjacent cells carry opposing orientations. The midpoints of these edges are marked as candidate points (red dots in (c)). We fit an on-scalp curve through these candidates to obtain the final partition line (d). Finally, any guide strand that crosses the partition is reassigned to its correct side (e), yielding the consistent parting visible in the resulting guides.

Fig. 8. Guide generation. We begin by binding the centerline of each card to form an initial set of guide strands (a). We then sample a sparser set of unbound root candidates, trace each one along the scalp’s normal direction until it hits the nearest card, and continue the remainder of the strand along that card’s orientation (b). The resulting guide set $\mathcal { G } ^ { \star } = \mathcal { G } \cup \mathcal { G } ^ { e x t r a }$ shown in (c) is then smoothed near the roots to reduce kinks, yielding the final guide output (d).

Fig. 9. Wrapper relaxation. We construct the SDF of the hair volume Ω, which encloses the thickened cards and the space between the hair cards and the bust (a). Each guide’s wrapper is initialized as a thin spindle that partitions the scalp region (b). After relaxation (c-d), every wrapper represents the volumetric occupancy of its wisp, and together they form a non-overlapping partition of the whole volume of the hairstyle.

## 5.2 Comparison with Alternative Hair Synthesis Methods W<sub>e</sub> <sub>compare</sub> <sub>our</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>syn</sub>th<sub>es</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r</sub> f<sub>rom gu</sub>id<sub>e</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rs.</sub>

Comparison with Conventional Interpolation Methods. Given the <sub>g</sub>uide hairs <sub>g</sub>enerated in Sec. 4.3<sub>,</sub> two inter<sub>p</sub>olation schemes are widel<sub>y</sub> used in traditional industrial <sub>g</sub>roomin<sub>g</sub> s<sub>y</sub>stems<sub>,</sub> such as Ma<sub>y</sub>a XGen and Houdini<sub>,</sub> to <sub>g</sub>enerate full hairst<sub>y</sub>les from <sub>g</sub>uide <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>sc</sub>h<sub>emes</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>sm-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpo</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ump-</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpo</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> I<sub>n pr</sub>i<sub>sm-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpo</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on, eac</sub>h d<sub>ense s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d root is inter<sub>p</sub>olated from a trian<sub>g</sub>ular <sub>p</sub>rism formed b<sub>y</sub> nei<sub>g</sub>hborin<sub>g</sub> <sub>g</sub>uide strands. In clum<sub>p</sub>-based inter<sub>p</sub>olation<sub>,</sub> each strand is inter<sub>p</sub>olated from a sin<sub>g</sub>le nei<sub>g</sub>hborin<sub>g g</sub>uide usin<sub>g</sub> frame trans<sub>p</sub>ort. How-<sub>ever, ne</sub>ith<sub>er sc</sub>h<sub>eme pro</sub>d<sub>uces usa</sub>bl<sub>e resu</sub>lt<sub>s w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>or-</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>p</sub>ost-editin<sub>g</sub>. Prism-based inter<sub>p</sub>olation blends across <sub>g</sub>uides with conflictin<sub>g</sub> features (Fi<sub>g</sub>. 18 (c)), which causes wides<sub>p</sub>read mesh <sub>p</sub>en-<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>genera</sub>t<sub>es s</sub>t<sub>ran</sub>d<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n reg</sub>i<sub>ons w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld instead contain <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s or follow a s<sub>p</sub>ecific orientation. Clum<sub>p</sub>-based inter<sub>p</sub>olation <sub>p</sub>reserves wis<sub>py</sub> features well<sub>,</sub> but often <sub>p</sub>roduces <sub>non-smoo</sub>th <sub>an</sub>d <sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ibl<sub>y c</sub>l<sub>umpy resu</sub>lt<sub>s, w</sub>ith <sub>no</sub>ti<sub>cea</sub>bl<sub>e ar</sub>tif<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> at clum<sub>p</sub> boundaries (Fi<sub>g</sub>. 18 (b)). Fig. 21. Comparison with a Learning-based Image-to-strand Method. While DifLocks [Rosu et al. 2025] can generate reasonable results, our method is closer to the input, since we take richer geometric information into consideration.

Fig. 23. Texture-guided initialization. (a) One texture strip ofthe test asset, from left to right: its alpha mask, its hair-ID map, its normal map, its flow map, its tangent map (hue encodes the direction), and the strands traced along the tangent map, one color per strand. (b) The input cards, the cards with their textures, and our strand result. When an asset ships high-fidelity textures such as an ID map and a flow map, we use their tangent information to reconstruct strands that follow the on-card structure and density more closely. The result closely matches the rendered textured cards.

Fig. 24. Scalp Region Clustering and Semantic Labeling. We first perform bilateral clustering over the scalp region and then assign semantic labels based on the clustering result. The left image shows the symmetric normal map used for K-means clustering, which produces a symmetric partition ofthe scalp. We then use this partition to assign semantic labels to the scalp regions.

The user specifies replacement rules of the form r<sub>tar et</sub> ← r<sub>can</sub>d<sub>i</sub>d<sub>ate</sub>.
